[[P1202]]CHAPTER XXIX

Some Fundamental Doctrines of [[Purvamimāmsā|Pūrvamīmāṁsā]]

Some of the characteristic fundamental doctrines of the [[Pūrvamimāṁsā|Pūrvamīmāṁsā]] will now be set out with some references and a few [[renarks|remarks]] as to some of them, the views of [[Prabhakara|Prabhākara]] and his followers being also rarely referred [[to|to.]]

(1) Veda is eternal, self-existent, not composed by any author, human or divine, and is infallible. This is the core of the [[P, M,|P. M.]] system. Vide P. M. S. [[1. 1.|I. 1.]] 27-32 and [[Sabara|Śabara]] on [[1, 1.|I. 1.]] 5 (p. 53) and [[ślokavārtika|Ślokavārtika]] ([[Vākyādhikarana,|Vākyādhikaraṇa,]] [[verges 1953|verses]] 1 [[366 368|366-368]]). The argument briefly is; Veda is learnt at present and [[. was|was]] studied in [[tbe|the]] past also by students from teachers, that there is no evidence about the person who first composed it or who first studied it. If it be argued that such a reasoning may be put forward as regards the [[Mahabharata|Mahābhārata]], the reply is that people know that Vyāsa is the author of it. Similarly, the passages in [[Smrtis|Smṛtis]] and Purāṇas that [[Prajapati|Prajāpati]] created the Veda are merely [[artharadas|arthavādas]] ([[laudatory )|laudatory)]] not based on any evidence or perception and are meant only to convey the absolute [[autho ritativeness|authoritativeness]] of the Veda. If the connection between word and [[. songe|sense]] is eternal and not brought about by any person, the same reasoning holds good as to the Veda. This view is opposed to the view of the Naiyāyikas who hold that God is the author of the [[Voda|Veda]] or the interpretation of V. S. I. 1. 3 ([[Sastrayonitvāt|Śāstrayonitvāt]]) by [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] based on Bṛ. Up. [[II, 4. 10.|II. 4. 10.]] Manu [[I, 21 (|I. 21 (]]stating that Brahmā created from the words of the [[Voda|Veda]] the names and appropriate duties of all) seems to suggest that (according to

[[P1203]]Manu) the Veda was self-existent. Similarly, the Mahābhāṣya on Vārtika 3 on Pāṇini IV. 3. 101 (“tena proktam’) remarks2 that the Vedas were not composed by anyone, that they are eternal, that the meaning of the Veda is eternal, but the [[arrange ment|arrangement]] of the letters is not eternal and therefore we have different vedic texts like [[Kāthaka,|Kāṭhaka,]] Kālāpaka, Paippalādaka &c. The Smṛtis also sometimes state that there is no author of the Veda, that Brahmā remembers it and that Manu also has remembrance of Dharma in different Kalpas ([[Parāśarasmrti I, 21|Parāśarasmṛti I. 21]]).

In P. M. S. I. 1. 28 ([[‘Anityadarsanāc-ca’|‘Anityadarśanāc-ca’]]) the opponent of the eternality of the Veda puts forward such passages as ‘Babara Prāvāhaṇi (son of [[Pravāhapa|Pravāhaṇa]]) desired’ (Tai. [[8.|S.]] VII. 1. 10. 2) and ‘Kusuruvinda [[Auddalaki|Auddālaki]] desired’ (Tai. S. VII. 2. [[%.|2.]] 1), which mention men named [[Prāvāhani|Prāvāhaṇi]] and [[Auddalaki|Auddālaki]] (son of Uddālaka) that are mortals and argues that Veda did not exist before those mortals and is, therefore, not eternal. To this the Siddhānta replies in P. M. S. I. 1. 31 (‘param tu śrutisāmānyam’) that such examples are to be explained differently viz, ‘Babara’ is a onomatopoetic word meaning ‘rustling’ and [[Prāvāhaṛi|Prāvāhaṇi]] (from ‘pra’ and ‘vāhay’ causal of [[lah|vah]]) means the wind.

It may be noted that centuries before Jaimini and [[Yaska|Yāska]] there was a school of Vedic interpreters called [[‘Aitibāsikas’.|‘Aitihāsikas’.]] For example, [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 98. 5 and 7 refer to Devāpi, son of [[Rṣtiṣena,|Ṛṣṭiṣeṇa,]] and to [[Santanu.|Śantanu.]] Yāska (in Nirukta II. 10), starting with the words [[“tatra-itihāsamācakṣate’|‘tatra-itihāsamācakṣate’]] states that Devāpi and [[Santanu|Śantanu]] were brothers of the Kuru family and the younger brother [[Santanu wa8.crowned|Śantanu was crowned]] king ignoring the rights of the elder and that these verses refer to them. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 10 there is a dialogue between Yama and [[Yami|Yamī]] and Nir. V. 2 refers to the 8th verse of it. Those who hold that Veda is eternal would explain Yama as [[Aditya|Āditya]] and [[Yami|Yamī]] as ‘Night’. [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] III. 33 is a dialogue between the sage [[Visvimitra|Viśvāmitra]] and the rivers. The Nir. (II. [[25–27|25-27]]) explains verses 5-6 and 10 of that hymn from the [[Aitihānika|Aitihāsika]] point of view and states that Viśvāmitra was son of king [[Kusika|Kuśika]] and does not mention the Nairukta interpretation of these verses.

[[P1204]]About the [[Asvins|Aśvins]] the Nirukta (XII. 1) states several views viz. they are Heaven and Earth, or Day and Night, or the Sun and the Moon and remarks that according to the [[Aitihasikas|Aitihāsikas]] they were kings who had accumulated great [[morit.|merit.]] Probably the Nairuktas were divided among themselves and explained that the [[Asvins|Aśvins]] represented different natural phenomena. About [[Vrtra|Vṛtra]] (occurring in such verses as [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 32. 11) the Nairuktas (Etymologists) said in Nirukta II. 16 that the word means ‘cloud’, while the [[Aitihasikas|Aitihāsikas]] say that he ([[Vitra|Vṛtra]]) was an [[Agura,|Asura,]] son of [[Tyastr.|Tvaṣṭṛ.]] As to the hymn [[Rg. 1, 105|Ṛg. I. 105]] of 19 verses (in the first 18 of which the refrain is [[‘vittam me asya rodasi’)|‘vittam me asya rodasī’)]] the Nirukta IV. 6 states that the hymn was seen by Trita who was thrown into a [[well In|well. In]] [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VII. 33. 11 there is mention of [[. Urvasi|Urvaśī]] and Vasiṣṭha ([[Maitrā.Varupa|Maitrā-Varuṇa]]) as born of [[Urvasi|Urvaśī]] and Nirukta (V. 13-14) explains that verse and remarks that [[Urvasi|Urvaśī]] was an apsaras and [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 95 is a dialogue between Aila [[Purū ravas|Purūravas]] and [[Urvash.|Urvaśī.]] But the interpretation of the Nairuktas and [[Aitihasikas|Aitihāsikas]] on this story are not expressly set out. Probably the Nairuktas would explain [[Urvasi|Urvaśī]] as lightning and Purūravas as the roaring wind. The hymn [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 108 is a dialogue between [[Sarama|Saramā]] (Indra’s bitch) and the Paṇis and the Nirukta XI. 25 explains the first verse and remarks that there is a story (Ākhyāna) therein viz. that there was a dialogue between Saramā sent by Indra and the Paṇis who were Asuras. In all such legends the Nairuktas would insist that they are to be taken allegorically as referring to natural phenomena, while the Aitihāsikas would insist that there is historical basis in all these. Though it is not stated by the [[Ninukta|Nirukta]] expressly that the [[Aitibāsikas|Aitihāsikas]] do not hold the doctrine of the eternality of the Veda it implicitly follows from their explanations that they did not subscribe to that doctrine.

  1. The connection between word and sense is eternal.3 This is explained by the words of [[Sabara|Śabara]] (in n. 1953) that there is no person who could be put forward as [[baving|having]] brought about

[[P1205]]the connection of word and sense. Vide [[P. M. 8.|P. M. S.]] I. 1. 6-23, [[Sabara’s|Śabara’s]] bhāṣya thereon, the elaborate treatment in 444 [[yerses|verses]] in Ślokavārtika (pp. [[728-8'5|728-845]]) and [[Prakarana-pañoikā|Prakaraṇa-pañcikā]] pp. 133-140 (seventh [[Prakarana|Prakaraṇa]]). On the question “what does a word like ‘Gauḥ’ denote,” the [[P. M. 8.|P. M. S.]] replies that a word denotes [[‘aksti’|‘ākṛti’]] (or jāti) i. e. the universal, the class. Briefly, the [[Mimārgakas|Mīmāṁsakas]] hold that the word, the denotation and the relation of these two are eternal. Vide [[P. M, S,|P. M. S.]] I. 3. 30-35.

  1. The individual soul. The [[P. M. 8.|P. M. S.]] does not expressly affirm the existence of the soul in any [[sutra.|sūtra.]] [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] on V. S. III. 3. 53 refers to this fact, states that [[Sabara,|Śabara,]] the [[Bhāṣya kāra,|Bhāṣyakāra,]] propounded the existence of the soul and that the venerable [[Upavarga|Upavarṣa]] refrained from going into that question in his [[exposi tion|exposition]] of the [[Pārvamimamga|Pūrvamīmāṁsā]] with the remark that he would expound it when dealing with [[Sāriraka|Śārīraka]] (i. e. the [[Vedanta-sūtra|Vedānta-sūtra]]). It was probably due to this fact of the absence of a statement about the soul that the Pūrvamīmāṁsā was interpreted by some as favouring atheism. Kumārila complains that the [[Minārsā,|Mīmāṁsā,]] though not really atheistic, was put forward as Lokāyata (atheistic)4 by some and that therefore he endeavours (in the

[[P1206]]Ślokavārtika) to show that it treads the path of belief (in soul and another world). The souls are many, eternal, all-pervading (omnipresent) and different from the body, the senses and the mind. The soul resides in the body, is the agent [[( karl)|(kartṛ)]] as well as [[blɔkir|bhoktṛ]] (enjoyer), is of the nature of [[puro|pure]] consciousness, and is cognised by itself ([[svasamvedya|svasaṁvedya]]). Though the P. M. S. does not expressly deal with the existence of the soul, there are indications that the P. M. S. itself must have impliedly accepted the existence of the soul. The reward of many religious rites is Svarga and the P. M. S. refers to numerous Vedic [[paysages|passages]] where svarga is the reward of rites and assumes that the reward goes to the performer (e. g. in the [[adhiburana|adhikaraṇa]] III. 7. 18-20 beginning with ‘Śāstraphalam prayoktari’ that expounds the meaning of such vedic passages as ‘agnihotram juhuyāt [[svargakāmak’).|svargakāmaḥ’).]] [[Sabara|Śabara]] on I. 1. 5 expounds at great length the existence of the soul as different from the body, [[sepse3|senses]] &c. (vide particularly [[pp. - 60–72|pp. 60-72]] of Ānan. ed.). The Ślokavārtika [[devotos|devotes]] 148 verses to this subject and the Tantravārtika also briefly deals with it on P. M. S. II. 1. 5 (pp. 402-403). The [[Slokavāritka|Ślokavārtika]] ([[Atmavāda,|Ātmavāda,]] [[Verse|verse]] 148) has this interesting verse 5 ’the Bhāṣyakāra ([[Sabara|Śabara]]) desirous of refuting atheism has established here (i. e. in [[bbāṣya|bhāṣya]] passages) by reasoning the existence of the soul; the

[[P1207]]knowledge on this subject (viz. existence of the soul) becomes sound and enduring by the study of Vedānta passages’. The Padma-purāṇa states that Jaimini composed a vast, but useless Śāstra by expounding the view of there being no god.6

  1. God and the devatās in sacrifices. [[Sabara’s|Śabara’s]] position is that the Vedas were not created by God, nor was the connection between word and sense created by Him. The [[Prakararapancikā|Prakaraṇapañcikā]] also (pp. 137 ff) denies [[&|a]] creator for the whole universe. Kumārila’s position7 also is peculiar and startling. He says in the Ślokavārtika that it is difficult to prove that God first created the world together with Dharma and Adharma and the means of attaining these, words, [[sepses,|senses,]] and their relations and the Veda. Thus it appears that he does not expressly deny the existence of a supreme power or God but [[simpily|simply]] ignores such power or God. In spite of this he begins his Ślokavārtika with an obeisance to Śiva.8 The Nyāyaratnākara says that the verse can also be an apotheosis of Yajña. But in that case Kumārila would have to be charged with duplicity. It is better to say that the tradition of beginning a work with a [[mangala verso|maṅgala verse]] could not be discarded even by Kumārila.

The consideration of the question of what is meant by the [[deratā|devatā]] with reference to which an offering is cast into the sacred fire leads to startling results. Jaimini (in VIII. 1. 32-34) takes the position that in a sacrifice the havis (offering) is the principal matter while the devatā is a subordinate [[ono (guna)|one (guṇa)]] and that if there be a conflict between the havis and the devatā, the final conclusion is to be arrived at by relying on the havis. The reasoning is that the Veda connects the deity with the sacrificial act, as in ‘one desirous of progeny should offer an havis for Indra and Agni cooked on eleven potsherds and then Indra confers on him progeny’ (Tai. S. [[II, 2. 1. 1.|II. 2. 1. 1]]). Even though

[[P1208]]it is so, the phala (the reward) is conferred by the sacrifice and not by the deities (Indra and Agni in this case) and the words ‘Indra 9 and Agni confer progeny on the sacrificer’ are purely laudatory. P. M. S. IX. 1. 6-10 are very important in this connection. [[Sabara|Śabara]] quotes Vedic passages like [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 47. 1, III. 30. 5, VIII. 17. 8 (where Indra’s right hand, fist, neck, stomach and arms are mentioned), [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 95 10, [[VIIL,|VIII.]] 77. 4 (where Indra is said to have put in his stomach all foods and to have drunk thirty [[yessels|vessels]] full of soma), [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VIII. 32. 22 and X. 89. 10 where Indra is said to be lord of the world, of heaven and earth, waters and [[nountains|mountains]] and then remarks that these are all [[artharūdas,|arthavādas,]] though at first sight they appear to postulate that Vedic deities have bodies, eat and drink. The [[Sastradipika|Śāstradīpikā]] advances the argument that if the devatā had a body, ate, drank and was pleased, devatā will be anitya (ephemeral) and could not in that case be treated of in the Veda that is eternal. He further asserts that men of limited intelligence not knowing the real import of the Vedic passages may hold wrong opinions. [[Sabara|Śabara]] notes (on X. 4. 23) that there are various opinions as to who the [[Dovatās|Devatās]] are to whom either a hymn (sūkta) is addressed (as [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 94) or to whom ‘havis’ is directed by the Veda to be offered as in ‘one should offer to Agni an offering cooked on eight potsherds’; that the devatā becomes connected with the sacrifice not in its own nature but by the word used with reference to an offering and that where the Veda directs an offering to be made to Agni, one cannot use a synonym for Agni such as ‘[[Suci,|Śuci,]] Pāvaka, [[Dhumaketu,|Dhūmaketu,]] [[Krśanu,|Kṛśānu,]] [[Vaisvānara|Vaiśvānara]] or [[Sāpdilya.|Śāṇḍilya.]] Thus Devatā is a matter of words, as admitted by 10

[[P1209]]Śabara. The [[Prakaranapancikā|Prakaraṇapañcikā]] also states that there is no proof that a sacrifice [[(vāga)|(yāga)]] is a means of pleasing the deity and that to say that there is worship of a deity in a yāga is really metaphorical. From this and the [[proceding|preceding]] doctrine (No. 1) it follows that [[P. M. 8.|P. M. S.]], [[Sabara,|Śabara,]] and Kumārila discard the idea that the Veda is the word of God or that rewards of religious acts are due to the favour or grace of God. It is therefore that the Padmapurāṇa quoted (in note 1957a) above dubs Jaimini’s doctrine [[‘girīśvaravāda’|‘nirīśvaravāda’]] (Godless).

When the Veda says ‘one desirous of heaven should perform a sacrifice’, three ākāṅkṣās (expectancies) arise. The first is ‘what is to be brought about’; this is satisfied by the word svarga, stating the reward or the purpose. The 2nd is ‘by what means’ is the first to be brought about. That is satisfied by the meaning of the root ‘yaj’ (i. e. by yāga); the third is ‘what is the procedure or in what manner’; that is satisfied by the [[eata blishment|establishment]] of the sacred [[firus|fires]] and other rites that are described in the context of the passage ‘svargakāmo yajeta’. It is learnt from this passage that the reward or purpose (svarga) springs from (is brought about by) yāga and not by the [[dovatā.|devatā.]] 11

Later writers could not digest all these ideas about the deities in sacrifices. [[Venkatanātha|Veṅkaṭanātha]] (or [[Venkatadeśika,|Veṅkaṭadeśika,]] 1269–1369 A. D.) composed a work called [[‘Seśvaramimārsa’,|‘Seśvaramīmāṁsā’,]] in which he [[oriticizes|criticizes]] both [[Bhatta|Bhāṭṭa]] and Prābhākara schools and being an ardent Vaiṣṇava of the [[Ramānuja|Rāmānuja]] school attempts [[&|a]] synthesis of the two [[mimāmgās|mīmāṁsās]] and brings in the idea of God as the dispenser of the reward of sacrifices against the combined testimony of [[Sabara,|Śabara,]] Kumārila, [[Salikanātha|Śālikanātha]] and others. Vide Dr. [[Radha krisnan|Radhakrishnan]] in ‘Indian Philosophy,’ vol. II pp. 424-429 for ‘God and world according to [[Pūryanimāṁsā’.|Pūrvamīmāṁsā’.]]

  1. There is no real creation nor dissolution of the whole universe. The constituent parts may come and go but the universe as a whole has no beginning and no end. The [[descrip tions|descriptions]] of creation and dissolution are only meant to illustrate the power of [[daita|daiva]] (fate, destiny) and the vanity of human effort and urge men to perform duties enjoined by the Veda. Without any human effort the world may come into being and in spite of

[[P1210]]all effort it may come to an end. The world is real and has always been there and will go on for all time.12 Vide [[Slokavārtika|Ślokavārtika]] (V. 112-117), [[Prakaranapancikā|Prakaraṇapañcikā]] pp. 137-140 and [[Nyayaratna kara|Nyāyaratnākara]] on the former. The [[Slokavāratika|Ślokavārtika]] goes so far as to say ‘it must be admitted that all these (worlds &c.) existed before the creator, and again such a creator may be proved (by anumāna) to have been preceded by the [[Voda|Veda]] on account of his being an intelligent being, just as ourselves are [[praceded|preceded]] by the Veda.’

It would be noticed that the [[Mimāınsa|Mīmāṁsā]] idea of creation and dissolution is opposed to the [[Mahabharata|Mahābhārata]] and [[Gita|Gītā]] (X. 8 [[‘aham sarvasya prabbaro mattaḥ sarvam pravartate’).|‘ahaṁ sarvasya prabhavo mattaḥ sarvaṁ pravartate’).]]

  1. The doctrine of [[upūria|apūrva]] (invisible mysterious or subtle potency). The Veda lays down that one desirous of heaven should perform a sacrifice. But the reward heaven comes off only after a long time, while the sacrifice lasts only for a short time. There would thus be no direct connection between the sacrifice (the cause) and heaven (the reward) or purpose. It must be presumed from the Vedic injunction that there is some connecting link between the activity of man to perform a yāga and the reward. Before the principal and subordinate [[acta|acts]] in a sacrifice are performed men have no capacity for svarga and sacrifices (described in the [[texta)|texts)]] are also incapable of producing the effect ‘svarga’. The principal and subsidiary [[sots|acts]] in a sacrifice when carried out remove the incapacity and produce a certain capacity or potency for svarga. This must be admitted by all. Unless such a capacity exists there would be the [[unac ceptable|unacceptable]] conclusion that not doing the acts and doing them are on the same level. This capacity or potency either [[rosiding|residing]] in the man (agent) or arising from the sacrifice that is performed is called [[apūrvu|apūrva]] in this [[sāstra.|śāstra.]] It is true that this capacity cannot be proved by direct perception or other means of knowledge except by [[śrutārthāpatis.|śrutārthāpatti.]] When we are told that a man

[[P1211]]who is fat does not eat by day we presume, in order to account for this, that he must be eating food at night. Similarly, the Veda brings sacrifice and svarga together; we have to presume that sacrifice gives rise to a subtle potency, though the sacrifice itself comes to an end in a short time, and that this potency is the cause of bringing about the reward svarga and that we may look upon it as abiding in the soul of the sacrificer or as an invisible effect (a reaching forth of the yāga itself that has vanished).13 [[Mimāmsakas|Mīmāṁsakas]] do not admit that the results of religious acts are given by God. The [[V. S. III 2. 40|V. S. III. 2. 40]] states that this is the view of Jaimini ([[dharmam Jaiminir-ata eva|dharmaṁ Jaiminir-ata eva]]) and is opposed to the view of [[Bādarayana,|Bādarāyaṇa,]] [[Śhaṅkara|Śaṅkara]] and the [[Bbāmati|Bhāmatī]] that it is God who confers the reward. According to the [[Prakaranapanoikā|Prakaraṇapañcikā]] ([[p. 186 )|p. 156)]] the invisible force or potency is not in the agent but arises as a subtle form from the action itself. Four kinds of [[Apūrvalos|Apūrva]] 14 (with further sub-divisions) as regards Darśapūrṇamāsa sacrifice are stated by [[Madhavācārya.|Mādhavācārya.]]

The idea is that every rite as a whole produces an apūrva, and that each subordinate part ([[anga|aṅga]]) of the rite has also an [[(apūrva)|apūrva]] which is subordinate to the apūrva of the whole rite.

The Tantravārtika explains why it is called apūrva. The invisible potency was not in existence before the performance of

[[P1212]]the sacrifice and arises [[8s|as]] something new after its performance and hence apūrva has a purely etymological [[sepgo.|sense.]] 15]

If one holds the view that apūrva is some potency coming to reside in the performer of a sacrifice one’s views would be like those of some modern writers who hold that real prayer is not mere repetition of certain words deemed to be sacred, but it is an upward movement or a real increase in the intensity of spiritual power in the man who prays, it being immaterial to ask to whom he prays (vide W. James’ ‘Varieties of religious experience’ p. 467 ).

  1. [[Svatah-prūmānya|Svataḥ-prāmāṇya]] ([[self-Falidity|self-validity]] of cognition). It has already been stated that the [[pramūnas|pramāṇas]] are six (five according to Prabhākara). The P. M. system postulates that all cognitions as cognitions are intrinsically valid in themselves, do not require any extraneous help to establish their validity but [[in validity|invalidity]] of cognitions is ([[parath|parataḥ]]) established extraneously by showing that there was defect in the organ that produced the cognition or it is established later on that a particular cognition was wrong.16 Prabhākara goes much further and holds that every experience as such is valid and no experience can be said to be wrong or illusory.

  2. [[Starga.|Svarga.]] The ideas about heaven entertained by Jaimini, [[Sabara|Śabara]] and Kumārila are different from the description of heaven in the Veda and Purāṇas. Vide H. of Dh. vol. IV. pp.

[[P1213]]165-167 and 168-171 for descriptions of the joys of heaven in the Vedic literature, the Epic and the Purāṇas. From [[considera tions|considerations]] of space the remarks will have to be brief. In [[Rg. IX, 113, 7-11|Ṛg. IX. 113. 7-11]] the sage prays to Soma to place him in that immortal world where there is continuous light, where all desires are fulfilled, where there are joys of various grades. Heaven was deemed to be the place where the souls of valiant men that fought battles and sacrificed their lives [[wont|went]] ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VI. 46. 12) and in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X [[154. 2-4|154. 2-5]] the soul of the dead is asked to join those departed men that had become invincible by great tapas, that had died in battle, that had made gifts of a thousand cows, that had led a life of right and virtue and that were wise sages.

The Atharvaveda IV. 34. 2 and 5-6 [[say|says]] that in heaven there are many women, they [[got|get]] edible plants and flowers of various kinds, there are streams of ghee, honey and wine ([[sura),|surā),]] milk, curds and there are lotus ponds all round. The [[Satapatha|Śatapatha]] Br. (XIV. 7. 1. 32–33) states that the joys of heaven are 100 times greater than those on earth. Vide [[Macdonnell’s|Macdonell’s]] ‘Vedic Mythology’ pp. 167-168 and A. B. Keith’s [[‘Religion and philo sophy of the Veda’sto.|‘Religion and philosophy of the Veda’ &c.]] pp. 403-406 (1924). Even the Upaniṣads refer to the joys of heaven e. g. [[Chan.|Chān.]] VIII. 5. 3. speaks of two lakes in the world of Brahmā, aśvattha tree showering down Soma and the city of [[Bṛahmā|Brahmā]] [[calld|called]] Aparājitā; the [[Kausitaki|Kauṣītaki]] Up. (I. 3 and 4) elaborates this and adds that five hundred Apsarases go out to meet him (who reaches heaven), one hundred with garlands in their hands, one hundred with ointments, one hundred with perfumes, one hundred with garments and one hundred with fruits. Poets like Kālidāsa love to dwell upon the instantaneous arrival of the soul of the warrior whose head was cut off in battle and who was at once joined by an apsaras in heaven (vide [[Raghuvamsa|Raghuvaṁśa]] VII. 51 [[‘Vāmāógasamsaktasuran gunaḥ svam prtyat-kabandham samare dadarś&’).|‘Vāmāṅgasaṁsaktasuraṅganaḥ svaṁ nṛtyat-kabandhaṁ samare dadarśa’).]] The Purāṇas furnish graphic descriptions of the joys of heaven. Vide [[Brahma purāṇa|Brahmapurāṇa]] 225. 6, Padma II. 95. 2-5, Mārkandeya 10. 93-95, which enumerate Nandana garden, aerial cars with bevies of Apsarases, golden beds and seats, absence of sorrows, all pleasure &c. [[Sabara|Śabara]] on P. M. S. VI. 1. 1 refers to two popular views about svarga; one is, whatever gives pleasure to a person such as silken garments of fine texture, sandalwood, damsels sixteen years old is designated [[starga.|svarga.]] Another popular view is that svarga is a certain place where there is no heat nor cold, no hunger nor thirst, no dissatisfaction, no tiredness.

[[P1214]][[Sabara|Śabara]] and Kumārila say that popular 17 ideas about svarga are invalid, that the [[Mahabharata|Mahābhārata]] and [[Purīgas,|Purāṇas,]] being composed by men, need not be considered and that the Vedic descriptions of svarga are [[more|mere]] arthavādas for [[praise,|praise.]]

[[___P. M. S.|P. M. S.]] IV. 3. 15. provides that svarga 18 is the reward of all religious acts (such as Viśvajit) for which no express reward is provided by the texts. [[Sabara|Śabara]] states ‘happiness is svarga and all seek that’; an old [[verze|verse]] says ’that state of happiness in which there is no mixing of pain, which is not immediately overwhelmed by pain, that comes to one when one desires it, is called by the word [[srar|svar]] (svarga)’.

Medhātithi remarks that [[smitis|smṛtis]] sometimes state 19 that the gift of a single cow yields all rewards and relieves from sins with the result that fruits of great religious acts and of slight ones would be deemed to be the same but it should be [[under stood|understood]] that the rewards [[diffor|differ]] in their duration; otherwise, no one would engage in great or difficult rites.

Some Vedic rites expressly declare fruits of sacrifices that are other than svarga. For example, the [[Tai, 8.|Tai. S.]] provides one desirous of (plenty of) cattle should offer the [[catrd|citrā]] sacrifice’ ([[II. 4.6. 1.|II. 4. 6. 1-2]]) or one desirous of ([[leadership or control over )|leadership or control over)]] a village should offer the [[Isti|iṣṭi]] called [[Sangrahant’|Saṅgrahaṇī’]] ([[Tal.|Tai.]] S. II. 3. [[9.2.).|9. 2).]]

[[P1215]][[Sabara|Śabara]] says the Veda does not say that the result of such sacrifices cannot be had in this very life. This the [[Tupṭikā. 19|Ṭupṭīkā]] 20 expands in an interesting note. The remedy declared by the Veda for securing desired objects (son’s birth and the like) is bound to yield the result in this life or in the next. If a man has committed evil deeds in a previous life he has to undergo the effects of those [[sing|sins]] and while he is undergoing the effects of sins the fruits of sacrifices are postponed. But if the effects of sins have been extremely reduced [[be|he]] secures the fruits of kāmya rites in this very life. The words of the Veda laying down an injunction state only so much that the fruit of the rite performed does come about but they do not say that the fruit follows immediately ( after the rite is performed). Therefore, there is no definiteness (about the time when the fruit will follow). But svarga is to be enjoyed only in another life (as a fruit of rites performed in this life). It [[(avarga)|(svarga)]] means unequalled [[happin488|happiness]] and is achieved in a degree proportionate to the actions; but it cannot be enjoyed in this life, since men experience in this world every moment both happiness and misery. Every happiness is not the fruit of [[Jyotiṣtoma|Jyotiṣṭoma]] and every being does not perform [[Jyotistoma.|Jyotiṣṭoma.]] But some happiness does come to a man. Therefore, it (happiness ) is natural. The existence, however, of another body has to be imagined for the experience of unequalled [[happi ness,|happiness,]] there being no other reasonable explanation. That unequalled happiness does not come to a man unless he dies; therefore svarga is to be enjoyed in another life.

  1. [[Mokṣa (liberation):|Mokṣa (liberation).]] Neither the P. M. S. nor [[Sabara|Śabara]] nor Prabhākara deals with the topic of Mokṣa. Kumārila and

[[P1216]][[Prakaranapancikā|Prakaraṇapañcikā]] deal with it. They both say that liberation consists in not having to assume a body again.21 The [[śloka vārtika|Ślokavārtika]] states ‘one desiring Mokṣa should not do any [[aot|act]] that is forbidden or is [[Kumya|kāmya]] ([[proscribed|prescribed]] for those who desire an object like riches, son &c); he should perform obligatory rites ([[euch|such]] as agnihotra) and naimittika (like bath, japa and gifts on eclipse &c.) in order to avoid the sin that would accrue owing to [[non performance|non-performance]] of those two; if he does not desire the rewards of doing [[pitya|nitya]] and naimittika acts they will not come to him, as such a reward comes to him who seeks it. The results of [[aots|acts]] in a former life would be eliminated by undergoing them in the life in which he seeks liberation. This differs from [[Saṅkarācārya’s|Śaṅkarācārya’s]] position who holds on V. S. IV. 3. 14 that, as declared in the [[Svet.|Śvet.]] Up. III. 8, there is no other way to [[Moksa|Mokṣa]] except knowledge and realization of the Ātman. In [[bis bhāsys|his bhāṣya]] on the same sūtra he seems to attack 22 [[Kumarila’s|Kumārila’s]] position. According to Kumārila Upaniṣad passages about the knowledge of the Atman are only arthavādas as [[thoy|they]] convey to the performer the knowledge that he has a soul and that the soul has certain characteristics, while [[Śhaṅkara|Śaṅkara]] maintains (on V. S. I. 1. 1) that the phala, the subject of investigation and the Vedic exhortation (codanā) are different in [[Pūrvamināmsā|Pūrvamīmāṁsā]] and in [[Brahamaml māmsā.|Brahmamīmāṁsā.]] There exist [[smrtis|smṛtis]] that ridiculed the idea that the mere

[[P1217]]knowledge of the Self would lead to Mokṣa. For example, 23 the [[Br. Y. Y.|Bṛ. Y. Y.]] affirms that both knowledge and actions lead to [[Liberation,|liberation,]] that to say that knowledge alone leads to liberation is a sign of indolence, that out of the fear of bodily labour ignorant men do not want to engage in actions.

The doctrines of the early and principal writers on [[Pūrya mimāṁsā|Pūrvamīmāṁsā]] are rather quaint and startling. Their arguments about the eternality and self-existence of the Veda are fallacious and were not accepted even by other ancient Indian systems. Both Prabhākara and Kumārila have in their scheme no place for God as the dispenser of rewards or as the ruler of man’s destiny, as being pleased with men’s prayers. They do not expressly deny the existence of God, but they assign to God or the deities mentioned in the Vedic texts a secondary role or rather [[practi cally|practically]] no role at all. They raise [[yajia|yajña]] to the position of God and their dogmas about yajña seem to be based upon a sort of commercial or business-like system, 24 [[viz,|viz.]] one should do so many acts, dispense gifts to priests, offer certain offerings, observe certain ethical rules and other rules of conduct (such as not eating flesh, [[su bsisting|subsisting]] on milk) 25 and then the reward would follow without the intervention of God. There is hardly any appeal to religious emotions, there is no omniscient [[Boing,|Being,]] no Creator and no creation of the world. The [[Pūrvam māmsā|Pūrvamīmāṁsā]] no doubt lays emphasis on the [[rights and) duties|rights and duties]] of man in life. The other darśanas are far more concerned with the [[ques tion|question]] of escaping permanently from the world and with man’s destiny after death. The P. M. S., [[Sabara|Śabara]] and Kumārila, [[how ever,|however,]] make substantial contributions to the exegesis of Vedic

[[P1218]]texts. There are about three thousand quotations in [[Sabara’s|Śabara’s]] [[bhāsya,|bhāṣya,]] out of which several hundred have not been yet identified. At least one thousand of these are taken from the Tai. S. and Br. About twelve [[adhikarapas|adhikaraṇas]] are concerned with the [[Adhri. gupraiṣa|Adhrigupraiṣa]] (a long formula) [[wbich|which]] is quoted in H. of [[Dh,|Dh.]] vol. II. p. 1121 note 2504. Some [[adhikarapas|adhikaraṇas]] are devoted to the [[explana tion|explanation]] of certain words in that [[praisa|praiṣa]] [[e. g. PMS IX, 4. 22|e. g. P. M. S. IX. 4. 22]] explains urūka as ‘vapā’, IX. 4. 23-24 explain ‘praśasā’, [[IX. 4, 25-27|IX. 4. 25-27]] explain five words ([[syena,|śyena,]] [[śalā,|śaśā,]] kaśyapa, kavaṣa, [[srekaparna).|srekaparṇa).]] Similarly, X. 1. 32 explains the word bṛsuya as meaning [[‘bphat.’|‘bṛhat’.]] [[Sabara’s|Śabara’s]] and Kumārila’s stands on the individual soul and [[moksa|mokṣa]] confer on the P. M. system [[soine|some]] status as [[& philoso phical|a philosophical]] one. For a systematic and exhaustive study of [[Sabara’s|Śabara’s]] contribution to Vedic and [[post-vedic|post-Vedic]] exegesis, vide Dr. S. V. Garge’s citations in [[‘Sābara-bhāsya’|‘Śabara-bhāṣya’]] pp. 140-213 (Poona, 1952).

The doctrine that the Veda is eternal and of absolute and unquestioned authority has led to certain undesirable tendencies. Propounders of new doctrines make great efforts to show that there is Vedic authority for what they hold. For example, the [[gutras|sūtras]] in V. S. I. 1. 5-18 are concerned to show that the Upaniṣads do not postulate [[Pradhana|Pradhāna]] as the cause of the world, as the Sāṅkhyas imagine. [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] expressly states that the Sāṅkhyas explain Vedānta passages as favourable to their theories and therefore a refutation of their arguments was 26 provided in [[V, 8. I. 1. 5-18.|V. S. I. 1. 5-11.]] It has been shown above how followers of [[Sākta|Śākta]] worship try to interpret a verse like [[Rgveda V, 47. 4|Ṛgveda V. 47. 4]] ([[catvāra Im bibhrati &c|catvāra īm bibhrati &c.]]) as referring to the [[Sakta|Śākta]] doctrines and how [[treatisos|treatises]] dignified with the title of Upaniṣads were produced by [[sākta|Śākta]] writers such as the Bhāvanopaniṣad. [[Sabara|Śabara]] in his bhāṣya on [[PMS. 1. 1. 5|P. M. S. I. 1. 5]] states that the Vijñānavādi Bauddhas put forward [[Bṭ. Up.|Bṛ. Up.]] IV. 5. 13 (Vijñānaghana evaitebhyo [[bhūtebbyaḥ|bhūtebhyaḥ]] samutthāya tānyevānuvinaśyati na pretya sanjñāsti) as supporting their position. One of the most glaring examples of this tendency is the explanation of [[Rg. L. 141. 1-3|Ṛg. I. 141. 1-3]] by [[Anandatirtha,|Ānandatīrtha,]] also called [[Madhyācārya,|Madhvācārya,]] who in [[‘Mahābhārata-tātparya-nirṇaya’|‘Mahābhāratatātparyanirṇaya’]] claims to be the third [[incarna tion|incarnation]] of [[Vayu,|Vāyu,]] the other two being Hanūmat and [[Bhimasena,|Bhīmasena,]] one

[[P1219]]of the [[Pāṇdaves,|Pāṇḍavas,]] and who endeavours to interpret [[Rg. I. 141; 1-3|Ṛg. I. 141. 1-3]] as referring to these three [[avatāras. 1918|avatāras.]] 27 The words [[’*Madhvaḥ’|‘Madhvaḥ’]] and [[‘Mātariśva’|‘Mātariśvā’]] (meaning ‘wind-god’) occur in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 141. 3. This was deemed sufficient to claim that [[Madhya,|Madhva,]] the propounder of the Dvaita system, was mentioned in the [[Rgveda.|Ṛgveda.]] I do not know how Madhvācārya would have met the charge of Veda not being eternal if it referred to [[Bhimasena|Bhīmasena]] ([[who oven’|who even]] according to the traditional account in the Mahābhārata lived at the end of Dvāpara age i. e. about 5000 years ago) and to Madhva himself who lived barely seven centuries ago. The Veda would be later than those dates. The argument that this refers to a previous Kalpa would not be available to rebut the charge, as the Kalpa, Manvantara and Mahāyuga when Bhīma and Madhvācārya flourished and in the present day are the same. There was no pralaya (dissolution of the world) at the end of Dvāpara and Kaliyuga immediately set in. The Bhārata war was fought between Dvāpara and Kali (Ādiparva 2. 13) and Kaliyuga was about to start at the time of that war (vide Vanaparva ’etat kaliyugam nāmācirād-yad pravartate’ and Salya 60. 25 (prāptam Kaliyugam viddhi)’. It is on account of this pretention that he was very roughly handled by eminent writers like [[Appayya-dikṣita|Appayyadīkṣita]] who charges [[Madhya|Madhva]] with citing [[fabrioated|fabricated]] Vedic and other texts in support of his doctrines. Vide [[I.. A.|I. A.]] vol. 62 at p. 189 where the writer, Shri Venkatasubbiah, sets out over thirty works mentioned by [[Madhya|Madhva]] which he states occur nowhere else. M. M. [[Chinna swami,|Chinnaswami,]] who has edited (Benares, 1941) Appayya’s work in [[60 Verses|60 verses]] with the commentary of Appayya called [[Madhyamata vidhyamsana|Madhvamata-vidhvaṁsana]] and notes of his own, sets out on p. 4 thirty-six unknown works and the sūtras where they are cited by Appayya.

Having established that Veda is eternal and [[self existent|self-existent]] the [[Mimāınsakas|Mīmāṁsakas]] give free rein to their ingenuity, powers of [[reason ing|reasoning]] and logic. They have a logic of their own which governed not only the interpretation of Vedic passages but also of the [[Smartis|Smṛtis]] and the medieval works on [[Dharmaśāstra (including|Dharmaśāstra ( including]] law and procedure). As Colebrooke, one of the most accurate and level-headed of Western scholars of Sanskrit, said more than 130 years ago ’the disquisitions on the [[inimāinsā|mīmāṁsā]] bear therefore a certain resemblance to juridical questions; and in fact, the Hindu law being blended with the religion of the people, the same modes of reasoning are applicable and are applied to the [[ono|one]] as to the other. The logic of the [[Mimansa|Mīmāṁsā]] is the logic of the law; the rule of interpretation of civil and religious [[ordinances, Each|ordinances. Each]] case is examined and determined upon and from the cases decided the principles may be collected. A well-ordered [[arrange ment|arrangement]] of them would constitute the philosophy of law and this is in truth what has been attempted in the [[Mimadga’|Mīmāṁsā’]] ([[Miscella. neous|Miscellaneous]] Essays, Vol. I. pp. 316-317, Madras [[ed, of 1837).|ed. of 1837).]]

The first division of Vedic material is into Mantra and [[Brāhmana.|Brāhmaṇa.]] It has been already stated above (p. 1096 note 1776) that those are mantras that are recognized as such by the learned. [[P. M. 8.|P. M. S.]] (II. 1. 31-32) provides 28 that mantra is that which simply asserts (and is not [[hortatory )|hortatory)]] or (to say the same thing in another way) ’those are mantras that are so called on account of their asserting [[something’s|something’.]] On [[P. M. S. I 4. 1 Sabara|P. M. S. I. 4. 1 Śabara]] describes mantra as one that at the time when the procedure of [[&|a]] sacrifice is being followed, recalls or reminds or makes clear to the performer the matter that is prescribed [[0.g.|e. g.]] ‘I cut (the blades of) [[kusa|kuśa]] grass that is an abode for the deity’. This is only a general description of mantra and not an accurate [[defini tion.|definition.]] Mantras are not supposed to be useful in sacrifices by

[[P1221]]their mere utterance (uccāraṇa) but are really ‘abhidhāyaka’ (reminders of what is to be done or is being done). [[Sabara|Śabara]] remarks that lakṣaṇa is the only way for the recognition of mantras and not an enumeration of some characteristics of mantras as done by the Vṛttikāra such as these viz. 29 some end in ‘asi’ (thou art) or ’tvā’ as in Tai. S. I. 1. 1 ‘iṣe tvā’, prayer or wish (as in ‘āyurdhā asi’ in Tai. S. I. 6. 6. 1), praise (‘agnir mūrdhā divaḥ’ in Tai. S. IV. 4. 4). [[Sabara|Śabara]] points out that ‘asi’ and ’tvā’ occur even in the middle of mantras, the other characteristics such as blessing and praise are found in [[Brāhma - nas|Brāhmaṇas]] also. The M. B. P. remarks that there are (p. 66) one hundred kinds of mantras and that if we take into account the fourteen Vedic metres and their sub-varieties there would be 273 different varieties of ṛk mantras alone (p. 67). There are certain texts regarded as mantras (e. g. ‘Vasantāya kapiñjalān-ālabhate’ Vāj. S. 24. 20) which are not merely assertive but rather relate to the procedure of a yāga (in the case of Vāj. S. 24. 20, of Aśvamedha).

Mantras are classified under three heads viz. Ṛk, Sāman and Yajus. These are defined in P. M. S. II. 1. 35-37; the name [[șk|ṛk]] is applied to those 30 mantras that are divided into [[feat,|feet,]] metrical pādas (often) based on the meaning. Sāman is the name applied to Vedic mantras that are sung. It has been established in P. M. [[S:|S.]] VII. 2. 1-21 and IX. 2. 1-2 that the mantra 31 texts are not called Sāman but only the [[musio,|music,]] that

[[P1222]][[giti|gīti]] is an action which manifests various tunes owing to the internal effort made by the singer and in order to bring about the musical effect the singer has to resort to modifications of the letters of the ṛk, to disjunction (of parts of it), to omission (of [[&|a]] letter), to repetition, to pauses, to 32 stobha. In [[VII.2.|VII. 2.]] 1-21 the PMS establishes that the words ‘Rathantara-sāman’, [[‘Bphat sāman’|‘Bṛhat-sāman’]] refer only to the music and not to the ṛk or basic text set to music. Yajus means those Vedic mantras that are neither [[k nor sūman.|ṛk nor sāman.]] There is another word ’nigada’ applied to certain mantras that are addressed to others as directions to do certain acts and are uttered loudly such as [[‘agaidagain vihara’,|‘agnīdagnīn vihara’,]] [[‘proksapīrāsādaya,|‘prokṣaṇīrāsādaya’,]] ‘idhmābarhirupasādaya’. They are Yajus (i. [[e,|e.]] in prose), the only difference being that they are loudly uttered (in order that the persons to whom they are addressed may [[bear|hear]] them), while ordinary Yajus is uttered inaudibly. Vide P. M. S. II. 1. 38-45 for discussion on Nigadas and [[Mai. 8. HII, 6.5|Mai. S. III. 6. 5]] for [[‘uccair-pcā kriyata uccaiḥ sāmopāmśu yajuṣā’.|‘uccair-ṛcā kriyata uccaiḥ sāmopāṁśu yajuṣā’.]]

Mantra and [[Brābmapa|Brāhmaṇa]] constitute the Veda. The [[P.M. S.|P. M. S.]] 33] states that all those parts of the Veda that are not or cannot be called Mantra constitute Brāhmaṇa. [[Sabara|Śabara]] notes that the [[Vettikāra,|Vṛttikāra,]] in order to enable students to decide what texts are Brāhmaṇa texts, pointed out certain characteristics found in Brāhmaṇa texts such as abounding in the word ‘iti’ or [[‘ityāba|‘ityāha’]] (so it is said), [[anegdote,|anecdote,]] stating the reason for an injunction, etymology, condemnation, praise, doubt, injunction, instance where another did something of the [[game|same]] kind, what happened in

[[P1223]]past ages, changing the meaning after considering the context. 34 [[Sabara|Śabara]] also quotes two verses summarising under ten heads the characteristics of Brāhmaṇa texts, points out that all this is only illustrative and that the characteristics mentioned by the [[Vșttikāra|Vṛttikāra]] sometimes occur in mantras also, such as ‘iti’ in [[Rg, X. 119. 1,|Ṛg. X. 119. 1,]] [[‘ityāha’ in Rg. VII, 41, 2,|‘ityāha’ in Ṛg. VII. 41. 2,]] [[‘akhyāyika’ as in Rg. I. 116. 3,|‘ākhyāyikā’ as in Ṛg. I. 116. 3,]] reason (hetu) in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 2. 4. The [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] alone contains over ten thousand mantras. Hardly one-third of them are employed in all the Vedic rites. The rest are employed in japa. Besides, the other Vedas also have thousands of mantras. Therefore, no formal definition of mantra is attempted and all that is stated is that mantras are those which have been recognised as such by the learned. 35

Brāhmaṇas are attached to each Veda, such as the Aitareya and [[Kausitaki|Kauṣītaki]] Brāhmaṇas to the [[Rgveda,|Ṛgveda,]] the [[Taittiriya|Taittirīya]] to the [[Krsna|Kṛṣṇa]] Yajurveda, the [[Satapatha|Śatapatha]] to the [[Sukla|Śukla]] Yajurveda, the [[Tāṇdya|Tāṇḍya]] to the [[Samaveda|Sāmaveda]] and Gopatha to the Yajurveda. The Brāhmaṇas contain the oldest known specimens of [[probe|prose]] in Indo-European languages, though prose formulas (probably earlier in date than those in the Brāhmaṇas) are found in the [[Kṭsṇa|Kṛṣṇa]] and [[Sukla|Śukla]] Yajurveda [[Samhitās.|Saṁhitās.]] They are the primary source of information about sacrifices, ritual and priests. They contain numerous myths and legends put forward for illustrating ritual and sacrifices. They often mention contests between gods and asuras (titans) and abound in etymologies. Their contents can be divided into two broad classes, viz. injunctive [[(hortatory )|(hortatory)]] passages (vidhis) and explanatory (or laudatory) passages [[( arthavādas).|(arthavādas).]] The scope and purpose of [[arthavadas|arthavādas]] will be explained later on. But one important point to be noted is that the [[Mimāṁsakas|Mīmāṁsakas]] are strongly opposed to holding that any part of the [[Voda,|Veda,]] even the smallest, is useless or meaningless.

It is now time to see how the [[Mimāmsakas|Mīmāṁsakas]] deal with the contents of the [[Voda.|Veda.]] The extant Vedic literature is of

[[P1224]]enormous extent and of greatly varied [[content, Once|content. Once]] it is admitted that the Veda is self-existent and not the work of any author, [[bugan|human]] or divine, every sentence in it apparently becomes of infallible authority. The Veda being the only means of knowing Dharma, the [[Mimimsakas|Mīmāṁsakas]] had to accept that whatever the Veda says was authoritative and binding on them, as [[Sabara|Śabara]] 36 admits. But many Vedic texts are apparently in conflict with each other and often are in conflict with ordinary experience. A [[fow|few]] striking instances may be given to show how difficulties arose. The Tai. S. V. 2. [[2.7|2. 7]] and Mai. S. state ‘one [[ghould|should]] not pile the fire altar on (bare) earth, nor in the sky, nor in heaven’. 37 No one can build a fire altar in the sky, nor in [[heaven, The|heaven. The]] Veda apparently prohibits something which is quite impossible and therefore this prohibition seems at first sight senseless. The Tai. Br. (III. 8. [[10.5)|10. 5)]] states that by offering the Pūrṇāhuti the performer secures all desired [[objecte.|objects.]] If [[Pārṇāhuti|Pūrṇāhuti]] yields all objects, then one need not perform any further rites of Agnihotra &c. Does the Veda mean this? The Veda contains stories and legends about individuals e. g. [[Tai, S.|Tai. S.]] mentions Babara [[Prāvāhasi|Prāvāhaṇi]] who desired to be an eloquent speaker and therefore performed [[&|a]] sacrifice called [[Pancarātra|Pañcarātra]] and secured his object. The Veda would then be later in time than this Babara and this would forfeit the claim of being eternal [[&o.|&c.]] Therefore, [[Sabara|Śabara]] states that a story that never happened is described for the sake of praise. This may be an escape from a tight corner but the explanation does not redound

[[P1225]]to the credit of the Veda which is here admitted to have manufactured a non-existent story [[fcr|for]] pushing up one of its injunctions; besides, if people come to know from what [[Sabara|Śabara]] says that the story is false, people would not be induced to go in for performing that rite. A true story might encourage persons to follow the prescription of the Veda in this case. The [[Tantravartika|Tantravārtika]] (p. 125) on this explanation of [[Sabara|Śabara]] tries to meet the objection raised here. Sometimes Veda is divided into three portions viz. Vidhi, [[Arthavada|Arthavāda]] and Mantra, names of yāgas like [[udblid|udbhid]] and [[visvajit|viśvajit]] being placed under vidhi. The Ślokavārtika refers to this threefold division in its last verse. 38 Though the whole Veda is the only means of right knowledge about what Dharma is i. e. what should be done and what should not be done, the different parts of the Veda are not all directly related to right knowledge about Dharma. Large portions of the Veda are related only mediately to the principal part. 39 In one place [[Sabara|Śabara]] very [[succintly|succinctly]] but clearly defines the three classes of Vedic texts and illustrates them. The Veda is also divided into five parts viz. vidhi, [[arthavada,|arthavāda,]] mantra, [[namadheya|nāmadheya]] and [[prati ṣedha.|pratiṣedha.]] These five have already been mentioned above. Here some details will have to be added on each of these. Vidhi is an injunction (or exhortation) that is meaningful on account of enjoining a matter that has [[&|a]] (useful) purpose and it prescribes

[[P1226]]something that does not follow (or is not established by) from any other authority. [[Sabara|Śabara]] himself describes in many places what vidhi means. For example, the injunction ‘one desirous of (attaining) Heaven should offer Agnihotra’ prescribes (the [[per- . formance|performance]] of) [[boma|homa]] that does not follow from any other authority and that has a beneficial purpose, its meaning being ‘by the [[Agni. hotra|Agnihotra]] offering one should effect (the attainment of) [[svarga’, Where.|svarga’. Where,]] however, a rite is otherwise established, all that can be done is only to enjoin an accessory with regard to it. Thus in the [[sentonce|sentence]] ‘one should offer an oblation with curds’ the homa being already established by the words ‘one should offer Agnihotra (if one desires heaven)’ all that is enjoined is the offering of curds with regard to it, the meaning being ‘by curds one should effect the oblation’. Vide [[Tup-tikā|Ṭup-ṭīkā]] on P. M. S. VI. 3. 17 and M. N. P. p. 17 ([[B.O. R. I|B. O. R. I]] ed.). 40

The collection of [[vidbis|vidhis]] in the Vedic texts forms the core of the Veda and refers to many specific rites. The central [[olemont|element]] in a vidhi is the verb or verbal form, which will be analysed later on. The question is: how is one to recognize a vidhi. [[Sabara|Śabara]] quotes a verse which those who know the propositions about the meanings of words and sentences declare traditionally, viz. in all the Vedas the fixed (or [[defipite )|definite)]] sign of a vidhi is the words ‘one shall do’, ‘it should be done’, ‘it ought to be done’, ‘it should be so’, [[‘it ought to be so!. 192From|‘it ought to be so’.]] 41 From this it follows that a vidhi is ordinarily couched in the optative form (called [[vidhi ling|vidhiliṅ]] in Sanskrit), and that a verb in the present tense cannot ordinarily be taken [[48|as]] laying down a vidhi. 42 But sometimes a vidhi may be inferred even from a text in which the verb is in the present tense. For example, there is a [[vedic|Vedic]] text occurring in the [[Mahāpitryajña|Mahāpitṛyajña]] which states ‘he should follow holding the

[[P1227]]fuelstick below the handle of the ladle in the [[pitryajña,|pitṛyajña,]] since the performer of rites for the gods holds the fuel-stick above the [[handle. 1991|handle.]] 43 This has been construed as a [[vidbi|vidhi]] (exhortation) and not as [[& more arthayāda.|a mere arthavāda.]] Another example is that of Rātrisattras (Soma sacrifices occupying more than twelve [[days ).|days).]] A passage in the context of Rātrisattras states ’those who perform rātrisattra attain stability (or fame), are endowed with brahma [[lustro|lustre]] and eat or possess food’. This looks like a mere praise (arthavāda) of the performance of rātrisattras, but really it is a vidhi about the reward of rātrisattra mentioned in the above passage and states an exception to the rule that svarga is the reward of a rite in which no reward is specified by the [[Vedic. texts.|Vedic texts.]] This is employed in the Mit. on [[Yaj. III, 226|Yāj. III. 226]] which states that the sin committed through ignorance is removed by the performance of penances. Ordinarily, a sinful act is deemed to be destroyed by undergoing the penalty that God may inflict, but [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] states a special rule. Medhātithi on Manu V. 40 (stating that animals, birds and herbs dying by being offered in yajñas reach higher levels) says that this is merely an [[arthavada|arthavāda]] and no vidhi can be imagined from this by the maxim of Rātrisattra. 44 Vide [[Par,|Par.]] M. (I. 1. p. [[149 )|149)]] which holds that an

[[P1228]]adhikāravidhi about one desirous of stability has been assumed from the passage about rātrisattra. Raghunandana in Ekādaśītattva (p. 28) explains the sūtras (P. M. S. IV. 3. 17-19) and illustrates this nyāya.

Following the Vedas, the Smṛtis also lay down numerous vidhis by means of verbal optative forms or participles ending in ‘ya, tavya &c.’. For example, vide Manu IV. 25 ‘agnihotram ca juhuyāt’ and XI. 53 (‘caritavyam-ato &c.’) as instances of the two ways. From different points of view vidhi is classified in various ways. One classification is into four, Utpattividhi (originative injunction), Viniyogavidhi (applicatory), prayoga vidhi (performance), adhikāra-vidhi (eligibility). Utpattividhi is one which shows in a general way the nature of the rite, as in ‘agnihotram juhoti’ (he offers the agnihotra oblation); an applicatory injunction is one which conveys the connexion of a subsidiary matter with the main rite as in ‘dadhnā juhoti’ (he offers oblation with curds) and this is dealt with in the 3rd chapter of the P. M. S.; a prayogavidhi is an injunction that governs the order of parts in a rite and suggests non-delay or promptness in the performance, though often times it is implied rather than expressly stated. This is considered in the chapters IV and V of the P. M. S. An Adhikāravidhi (injunction of qualification or eligibility) is one that points to the ownership of the fruit of an action as in ‘svargakāmo yajeta’ (he who desires to secure heaven should perform a yāga). 45 This is the subject of the 6th chapter of P. M. S.

[[P1229]]Another and an important classification is apūrva-vidhi (injunction of something quite [[now|new]] and not otherwise [[establish ed|established]] as in [[‘gvargakāmo gajeta’),|‘svargakāmo yajeta’),]] [[niyamavidbi|niyamavidhi]] ([[(&|a]] restrictive injunction) as in ‘he pounds the rice’ and [[parisankhyāvidhi|parisaṅkhyāvidhi]] (an injunction in form where two alternatives being [[simul taneously|simultaneously]] possible one is precluded). The Tantravārtika defines these three in a well-known verse.46 For [[&|a]] sacrifice a plot of land is required which may be quite flat or may have ups and downs. Here there are two alternatives only and both cannot be availed of at the same time (i. [[e,|e.]] a man cannot perform a sacrifice at the same time on even as well as on uneven ground). Therefore, [[‘same dese yajeta’|‘same deśe yajeta’]] (one should offer a sacrifice on an even spot) is a niyama (it restricts the performance to flat land only) and excludes the use of uneven land for [[&|a]] sacrifice. ‘Five five-nailed animals are to be eaten’. This is [[& parisankhyā.|a parisaṅkhyā.]] This sentence is not a vidhi, as eating flesh is already established by men’s appetites. Nor is it a niyama, [[sinco|since]] one can eat the flesh of five-nailed animals as well as of other animals at the same time. It is a [[parisankhyā. I, e,|parisaṅkhyā, i. e.]] it prohibits the eating of the flesh of animals other than of five five-nailed animals. In form the sentence is a vidhi (as it employs the word [[‘bhaksyāḥ’|‘bhakṣyāḥ’]] (which is optative or potential passive participle), but in substance it is a prohibition of eating the flesh of animals other than the five five-nailed ones. The

[[P1230]]word [[parisankhyā|parisaṅkhyā]] occurs in P. M. [[8|S.]] X. 7. 4 and 7 and [[Sabara|Śabara]] notes that it is liable to three faults.47

[[Dharmasastra|Dharmaśāstra]] writers make frequent use of the doctrine of niyama and [[parisankhyā.|parisaṅkhyā.]] Medhātithi on Manu III. 45 ([[tuka. lābhigāmi syat|(ṛtukālābhigāmī syāt]]) has a long note on niyama and [[parisankhya,|parisaṅkhyā,]] quotes the verse of Tantravārtika and explains the passage about five five-nailed animals. The Mit. also on [[Yaj. on|Yāj.]] on I. 79 (tasmin yugmāsu [[sainviset|saṁviśet]] ’the husband should approach the wife on every even night next to the 4th night up to the [[¡16th|16th]] after her monthly illness starts) and on I. 81 holds a lengthy discussion whether there is a [[vidbi|vidhi]] or niyama or [[parisankhya.|parisaṅkhyā.]] The Mit. defines the three in prose, cites examples and tells us that some thought that there was only a [[parisankhyā,|parisaṅkhyā,]] while [[Bbāruci|Bhāruci]], Viśvarūpa, and others (including the Mit.) hold that there is a niyamavidhi only in I. 79 and 81. [[Ap. Dh. S. II, 1. 17|Āp. Dh. S. II. 1. 17]] deals with the same subject as Yāj. [[I,|I.]] 79 and 81 and Haradatta says it is a niyama, while others thought it was a [[parisankhyā: and that|parisaṅkhyā and that]] in any case it is not a pure vidhi. On Gautama V. 2 Haradatta comments that the ācārya (i. e. Gautama) holds that there is a [[parisankhyā|parisaṅkhyā]] only (the sūtra is [[‘garvatra vā pratisiddhavarjam ‘|‘sarvatra vā pratiṣiddhavarjam’]]). Compare [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] I. 81 ([[yathākāmi|yathākāmī]] bhaved-vāpi &c.), on which the Mit. emphatically says that in both Gautama and Yāj. there is a [[restriotive|restrictive]] rule (niyama). Gautama prescribes that a brāhmaṇa should eat at the houses of persons of the three higher classes that are commended for the performance of their duties and should receive gifts also from such persons. Haradatta treats these two rules as 48[[parisankhyāvidhis,|parisaṅkhyāvidhis.]] [[Ap. Dh. 8. II.|Āp. Dh. S. II. 1. 1. 1-2]] provides the rules of conduct for husband and wife after their marriage, the first being ’taking food two times (morning and [[evening).|evening)’.]] Haradatta takes it as a [[parisankhya,|parisaṅkhyā,]] meaning that eating [[&|a]] third time is prohibited (but they may or may not eat twice in the day), while others take it as a niyama meaning ’they must eat twice in the day’.

[[P1231]]Niyamavidhis are of three classes, viz. those concerned with pratinidhis (substitutes), those about pratipatti ([[concluding aot|concluding act]] or final disposal of certain materials employed in a sacrifice), and those that are concerned with matters other than the preceding two. The [[Tāpdya-brāhmaṇa|Tāṇḍya-brāhmaṇa]] says 49 ‘if one is not able to obtain the soma plant, one may extract juice from [[putikas’. Jai, III, 6. 40|pūtīkas’. Jai. III. 6. 40]] and VI. 3. 13-17 deal with this matter and Jai. and [[Sabara|Śabara]] prescribe that if no soma plant is available in a soma sacrifice, the sacrificer should substitute [[putikas|pūtīkas]] for Soma and no other substance, even if the latter [[bo|be]] more similar to Soma, can be used.50 The word pratipatti is used in several sūtras by Jai; vide IV. [[%.|2.]] 11, 15, 19, 22). The casting into water of all utensils and things smeared with [[some|soma]] (such as the [[drogs|dregs]] left after Soma is extracted, the stones, two wooden boards and the post of [[adumbara|udumbara]] in the midst of the sadas) at the time of the final purificatory bath ([[avabhrtha)|avabhṛtha)]] in [[Jyotiṣtoma|Jyotiṣṭoma]] is said to be pratipattikarma (P. [[M S.|M. S.]] IV. 2. 22). This terminology is employed in Dharmaśāstra works. Manu (III. 262-263) provides that out of the three [[pindas|piṇḍas]] (balls of boiled rice) that are offered to the three male ancestors of the performer of [[$rāddha|śrāddha]] the wife desirous of a son should eat the middle [[pinda|piṇḍa]] (meant for the paternal grandfather) and Devala provides that the [[piṇdas|piṇḍas]] may be given to a brāhmaṇa or may be eaten by [[&|a]] goat or a cow or cast into fire or water.51 [[(This|This]] is the pratipatti of the piṇdas according to Aparārka (on Yāj. I. 256) and the Sm. C. II p. 486. Vide H. of Dh. vol. IV pp. 480-481. The word pratipatti is opposed to arthakarma. For example, in [[Tai. 8.|Tai. S.]] we read ‘after the soma plant is bought, he gives the staff to the [[Maitrāvarupa|Maitrāvaruṇa]] priest’. Here the staff had been first given to the performer (Yajamāna) at the time of [[Diksa|Dīkṣā]] and then transferred to the [[Maitrāvarupa|Maitrāvaruṇa]] who has several [[uges|uses]] for it, viz. he can walk with its help in darkness, may enter water, can prevent cows and snakes from coming near him and he can

[[P1232]]support himself on it. So this is [[difforent|different]] from pratipatti, where the substance is finally disposed of and no further use is made of it. This is dealt with in P. M. S. IV. 2. 16-18. This (giving of the staff) is arthakarma as opposed to pratipatti-karma. This is mentioned in [[Tai, S. VI. I. 4.2|Tai. S. VI. 1. 4. 2]] ([[krite|krīte]] some [[maitrāvaruṇāya dandam|maitrāvaruṇāya daṇḍaṁ]] prayacchati &c.). Another example of pratipatti is the casting of the horn of the black antelope on the [[cūtvāla|cātvāla]] ([[Tai. S. VI, I. 3.8|Tai. S. VI. 1. 3. 8]] and PMS IV. 2. 19). PMS XI. 2. 66-68 deal with an example of arthakarma. Cremating a sacrificer when dead with his implements of sacrifice (mentioned in Tai. S. I. 6. 8. 2-3) is pratipattikarma of the implements (P. M. S. XI. 3. 34). Manu V. 167 provides that if the wife of an āhitāgni dies before him she is to be cremated with the sacred fires set up by him and with the sacrificial implements. An example of the third kind of niyama (which is not concerned with pratinidhi or pratipatti) is ‘one should take [[big|his]] meal,52 facing the east’. A man can take food facing any direction but this restricts him to the east alone. Here no question of a pratinidhi or of pratipatti [[arisons.|arises.]]

Vidhis are also divided into Kratvartha (for the rite) and Puruṣārtha (for the man) They are concerned with ‘Prayukti’ (motivating force) which is the subject of chapter IV of the P. M. S. P. M. S. IV. 1. 2 defines 53 puruṣārtha and [[Sabara|Śabara]] advances three interpretations of that sūtra, one of which is ‘(Puruṣārtha) is that matter on doing which follows the happiness of man, as its understanding is due to man’s desire for obtaining it (happiness) and puruṣārtha (man’s purpose) is not different [[(from happiness)’,|(from happiness)’.]] From this rather obscure and clumsy definition it appears to follow that puruṣārtha is what a man ordinarily undertakes for securing the reward of happiness, while kratvartha is that which helps in the accomplishment of [[purūṣārtha|puruṣārtha]] and does not itself directly yield any [[toward|reward]] to the performer. All principal [[sacri fices|sacrifices]] like Darśa-pūrṇamāsa are included under puruṣārtha, while under kratvartha are put all those auxiliary acts that have the

[[P1233]]purpose of accomplishing the principal rite itself e. g. the five 54 prayājas that are auxiliary to the [[Darśapūrpamāsa|Darśapūrṇamāsa]] are [[Kratvar tha,|Kratvartha,]] while [[Darbapūrṇamāsa|Darśapūrṇamāsa]] itself is puruṣārtha. The importance of this distinction lies in this that if what is kratvartha is not followed the rite itself becomes defective, while if what is puruṣārtha is not followed, the result is that the man himself becomes blamable or sinful but the undertaking or rite does not become defective. One set of the three interpretations of P. M. [[8.|S.]] IV. 1. [[%|2]] given by [[Sabara|Śabara]] is that it refers to the rules about the acquisition of wealth [[viz,|viz.]] that a brāhmaṇa should earn wealth by receiving gifts, a kṣatriya by conquest and a [[veibya|vaiśya]] by agriculture and the like (vide Gaut. X. 40-42, Manu X. 76-79). These are in the nature of niyamas. If the [[aoquisition|acquisition]] of wealth is kratvartha and, if one acquires wealth by means other than those prescribed by the Śāstra and performs a sacrifice with such wealth, the sacrifice itself would be defective and would not yield the desired reward. But if the acquisition of wealth be puruṣārtha, then, by whatever means wealth with which [[&|a]] yāga is to be performed be obtained, the sacrifice would not be affected. The Mit. on 55 [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] II. 114 quotes a passage from Guru Prabhākara (vide note 1947) which is quoted by the Dāyabhāga also (II. 67) but without name, while the Sm. C. II. pp. 257-58, Madanaratna (on Vyavahāra pp. 324-325) and [[Vyavabāraprakāśa|Vyavahāraprakāśa]] (p. 420) quote a similar passage from the Nayaviveka. Viśvarūpa also on Yāj. II. [[144|114]] holds that rules about acquisition of wealth are ‘puruṣārtha’. To acquire wealth is natural to man and acquisition of wealth does not depend upon [[Sastra.|Śāstra.]] Besides, every one can directly perceive that wealth when earned confers pleasure on the acquirer. Therefore, wealth is puruṣārtha and sacrifices which are performed with wealth are also puruṣārtha. The general rule is that all [[angas ( auxiliary|aṅgas (auxiliary]] rites) are kratvartha and all principal rites (like

[[P1234]][[Darbapūrpamisa,|Darśapūrṇamāsa,]] [[Somayaga)|Somayāga)]] are puruṣārtha; all texts [[presori bing|prescribing]] the rewards of rites are puruṣārtha. Some individual examples may be given.

In the [[Saṅkhāyana|Śāṅkhāyana]] Br. (VI. 6) after saying that certain observances (vrata) must be followed by the sacrificer, it is provided that he should not see the sun rising or setting. These observances are called ‘Prajāpativratāni’ by [[Sabara,|Śabara,]] and are declared by him to be puruṣārtha, the meaning being that the sacrificer should make a resolve not to see the rising or setting sun.

This distinction [[betwaon|between]] Kratvartha and Puruṣārtha is often taken over into Dharmaśāstra matters. For example, Yāj. I. 53 provides that one should marry a girl who is free from diseases, has a brother and who does not belong to the same gotra or pravara as that of the bridegroom. The Mit. 56 explains that the status of being a wife does not at all arise [[it|if]] the girl is a [[sapinda|sapiṇḍa]] or is of the same gotra or pravara as the bridegroom (i. [[e,|e.]] marriage itself is null and void), but a girl who has some disease when married becomes a wife, the only result being that there is a visible defect (such as a diseased wife is a [[. constant|constant]] source of anxiety and [[unbappiness).|unhappiness).]] Kullūka on Manu [[IL, 7|III. 7]] (providing that one should not marry a girl from a family in which [[provail|prevail]] diseases like phthisis, epilepsy, white and black [[leprosy, refers|leprosy) refers]] to this doctrine of [[Sabara|Śabara]] elaborated above. The Mit. on Yāj. III. 280 quotes a verse ‘a wise man should not marry a girl who has no brother, [[por|nor]] father, for fear that she may be a [[putrika|putrikā]] (daughter appointed as a son).’ Here the prohibition is like the prohibition of marrying a deformed girl i. e. it is a perceptible purpose. Therefore, the marriage would be valid i. e. the prohibition is puruṣārtha. Manu says (IX. 168) ’that is known to be an adopted son whom the mother or father might give (to another) with water in a time of distress [[&o.’.|&c.’.]] The [[Mit,|Mit.]] on [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] II. 130 quotes this verse and remarks that, as the word ‘distress’ is specifically mentioned, [[&|a]] son is not to be given in adoption to another when there is no distress and that this prohibition would affect only the giver (but not the act of adoption) i. e. this prohibition would be

[[P1235]]puruṣārtha and not kratvartha.57 It may be noted that the [[Vyavahāramayūkba|Vyavahāramayūkha]] disagrees and holds that the prohibition is [[kratyartha.|kratvartha.]] It may be said in a general way that those [[prescrip tions|prescriptions]] that have an invisible, spiritual or [[othor-worldly|other-worldly]] result or reward are kratvartha, while those that [[liave|have]] a visible result are puruṣārtha.

Before proceeding further it is necessary to analyse the notions underlying the word ‘yajeta’ used in such Vedic sentences as ‘one desiring heaven should offer [[& sacrifics’|a sacrifice’]] (svargakāmo yajeta). In the word ‘yajeta’58 there are two parts viz. the root ‘yaj’ (to sacrifice) and the termination (of injunctive third person singular). In the termination also there are two parts [[(elements ) viz,| (elements) viz.]] general verbal nature and injunctiveness (or injunctive force). General verbal nature is found in the ten [[lakūras|lakāras]] (verbal [[formg in ton|forms in ten]] moods and tenses), but injunctiveness is found only in the injunctive form. Both general verbal nature and injunctiveness express only bhāvanā (force that brings into being something, efficient force). Bhāvanā (lit.) means a particular operation or activity of an agent which conduces to the coming into being of the result ([[Lit.|lit.]] which is to come into being). And this bhāvanā is of two [[kirds, sābdi ( word force) and arthi|kinds, śābdī (word force) and ārthī]] (result-producing force).

It has been already stated that [[vidhia|vidhis]] are the very core of the Veda. The doctrine of [[bhāvana|bhāvanā]] is the very heart of vidhis and is therefore one of the most important doctrines of the [[mimamsa.|mīmāṁsā.]]

In ordinary life when a person says to another ’this should be done by you’, the prompting to do something comes from a person. But Veda has according to the [[Mimarisi|Mīmāṁsā]] no author, human or divine. Therefore, in a Vedic vidhi the [[promp ting|prompting]] arises from the injunctive or the optative form of the word; there is no person, human [[of|or]] divine, behind the [[injunotion;|injunction;]] hence the bhāvanā is said to be [[sābdi|śābdī]] (i. e. based on the word itself and not on the will or direction of a person). There-

[[P1236]]fore, [[sabdi-bhavanā|śābdī-bhāvanā]]59 is defined as a particular operation (or [[aotivity|activity]]) of an agent (here the word of the [[Voda)|Veda)]] that conduces to the activity of a man; and it is expressed by that part or element which is optative. It is called [[‘śābdi’|‘śābdī’]] because it is [[* sabdaniṣtha|śabdaniṣṭha]] (centred in the word of the Veda) and not [[puruṣaniṣtha|puruṣaniṣṭha]] (centred in a person). In a [[śābdibhāvanā|śābdībhāvanā]] there are three elements viz. (1) there is prompting of the agent to activity, (2) the injunction is the instrument and (3) the manner is furnished by the desirableness proclaimed by [[artha vāda|arthavāda]] passages. The [[sābdi-bhāvanā|śābdī-bhāvanā]] gives rise to [[arthi-bhiyanā.|ārthī-bhāvanā.]] In an [[arthi-bhāvanā|ārthī-bhāvanā]] (that aims at or seeks the artha, the phala) also there are three elements viz. (1) svarga is the phala that is to be brought about, (2) the means or instrument is [[yaga,|yāga,]] (3) there is also the procedure or method (itikartavyatā) of the yāga. All this is based on the P. M. S. sūtra II. 1. 1, on [[Sabara’s bhāsya|Śabara’s bhāṣya]] and certain important verses of the Tantravārtika quoted below. This whole discussion leads on to the idea of [[apūrra.|apūrva.]] Yāga lasts for a short time, while [[avarga|svarga]] comes to a man after death, which may be years after the performance of the sacrifice. What is the connecting link between yāga and svarga (the cause and the result)? It is the force or potency generated by [[yāgs|yāga]] that brings about svarga.

Briefly, the idea is; both the root and the affix together express the sense of the affix and as the bhāvanā is the [[prinopal|principal]] element in this, it is held to be the sense of the affix. There are numerous 60 ‘bhāvaśabdas’ such as yajati, juhoti, [[dadati,|dadāti,]]

[[P1237]]dogdhi, [[pinasti.|pinaṣṭi.]] These are of two sorts, viz. [[pradhana|pradhāna]] (principal) and [[gunabhūta,|guṇabhūta.]] 61 Those by which a substance is not intended to be produced or embellished (or made fit) for a religious rite, are expressive of principal karma (such as [[prayājas ),|prayājas),]] while those which are meant to produce a substance or to make a substance fit are [[gunabhuła|guṇabhūta]] (such as pounding rice grains or chiselling a piece of wood for making a sacrificial post, wiping [[stuc|sruc]] ladle). Therefore the latter kind of actions are called [[gunakarma.|guṇakarma.]] There are two kinds of verbal forms (1) those in which the form only conveys that the kartā (doer of the action) exists such as the forms ‘asti, bhavati, vidyate’; (2) there are others where not only there is the existence of the kartā but there is also the understanding of some activity that produces a result such as ‘yajati’ (meaning [[‘yagam karoti’),|‘yāgaṁ karoti’),]] [[’ dadati’|‘dadāti’]] (i. e. [[dānam|dānaṁ]] karoti), ‘pacati’ ([[pakam|pākaṁ]] karoti), ‘gacchati’ i. e. [[gamanam|gamanaṁ]] karoti). That is, in such cases the sense of karoti is also understood. Jaimini (in P. M. S. II. 1. 4) divides words into two classes viz. [[Namāni|Nāmāni]] (nouns) and [[Karmasabdoh|Karmaśabdāḥ]] (verbs). Under the [[firat Sabara|first Śabara]] would include pronouns and adjectives. The 2nd is called ‘ākhyāta’. [[Sabara|Śabara]] (on II. 1. [[3.) paraphrases (namani|3) paraphrases nāmāni]] as [[‘dravya-gunasabdah’|‘dravya-guṇaśabdāḥ’]] and remarks that in the [[sutra|sūtra]] (II. 1. 3) the word ’nāmāni’ is employed in the sense of [[‘dravyagunasabdah ’ . Sabara|‘dravyaguṇaśabdāḥ’. Śabara]] 62 remarks that [[dhatvartha|dhātvartha]] (the

[[P1238]]meaning of a verbal root) [[oan|can]] have no [[akānkṣa|ākāṅkṣā]] (expectancy) for Dharmas but it is the pratyayārtha that has [[akāñksā|ākāṅkṣā]] for the procedure (itikartavyatā).

We must now turn to the 2nd great division of Vedic [[texto,|texts,]] viz. Arthavādas. They are dealt with in P. M. S. first adhyāya, [[ind|2nd]] pāda. There are numerous [[Vodic|Vedic]] passages such as ‘he roared; because he roared he came to be called Rudra’ ([[Tai. $.|Tai. S.]] I. 5. 1. 1.), ‘Prajāpati cut out his own fat’ (Tai. S. [[II,|II.]] 1. 1. 4), ’the gods, having reached the sacrificial ground, did not know the directions’ (Tai. S. VI. 1. 5. 1.); ‘who knows whether one lives in the other world [[cr|or]] not’ [[(Tai. S. VI. I. 1. 1.);|’(Tai. S. VI. 1. 1. 1.);]] [[’thore|‘there]] should be no piling of fire altar on the earth nor in the sky nor in heaven’ (Tai. S. [[V,|V.]] 2. 7. 1). The objector says ‘you have yourself declared that enjoining religious rites is the purpose of the Veda’ (P. M. S. I. 1. 2). The above and similar passages do not serve any purpose in the matter of religious acts, they are therefore [[uselegg|useless]] and are non-eternal (or do not refer to any permanent matter). The reply is that these passages form one syntactical or connected whole with hortatory passages of the Veda and they [[serye|serve]] the purpose of commending (or glorifying) the hortatory passages. [[Sabara|Śabara]] cites on I. 2. 7 the passage ‘one desiring prosperity [[gbould|should]] sacrifice a white animal in honour of Vāyu; Vāyu indeed is the swiftest deity; he runs near Vāyu with the share proper (to Vāyu); he (Vāyu) leads the sacrificer to prosperity’. 63 These words form one whole passage; the first

[[P1239]]part [[‘vāyavyam..bhūtikāmaḥ’|‘vāyavyaṁ…bhūtikāmaḥ’]] is clearly a vidhi, as the word ‘ālabheta (in the optative form) shows. The latter portion is an arthavāda for the purpose of commendation. People are familiar with the fact that Vāyu is swift. Therefore, the latter portion (Vāyurvai &c.) merely repeats what is already well known (that is, it is an anuvāda). In sūtras 19-25 of I. 2 the P. M. S. considers certain passages that look like vidhis but are declared to be arthavādas. For example, (Tai S. II. 1. 1. 6) ’the sacrificial post is to be of udumbara wood; the udumbara wood is indeed vigour (or food or sap); cattle are vigour; by this strong (or juicy) post, he (sacrificer) secures cattle, for ’the securing of strength’. The objector says that this is a phalavidhi (an injunctive text about a result) as the words ‘ūrjo’varuddhyai’ contain the purpose and there is no word of commendation. The reply is that there is only commendation.

There are some passages in the Veda in which occur words like ‘hi’ (because) such as in ‘one should offer into fire the oblation with the winnowing basket, because food is prepared by means of it’ (Tai. Br. I. 6. [[5 ).|5).]] 64 The question that arises is

[[P1240]]whether this and other passages containing words denoting reason should be construed as arthavādas commending what is enjoined in the preceding sentence or whether as stating a reason for the injunction. The established conclusion is that they are commendatory. If the other view were adopted (viz. that śruti gives a reason for the vidhi) then it may be argued that the ladle, pan and other utensils also may be used for making an offering (not merely the [[sūrpa ),|śūrpa),]] as they also are required in preparing food. Raghunandana in Malamāsatattva p. 760 relies upon and explains this maxim with reference to Laghu-Hārīta’s words ‘cakravat parivarteta sūryaḥ kālavaśād yataḥ’. It should not be supposed that commendation (stuti) is the purpose of all arthavādas. In ‘he lays down anointed pebbles, ghee is indeed brilliance’ (Tai. Br. III. 2. 5. 12) a doubt arises about the material with which pebbles are to be anointed. That doubt is removed by the remainder of the passage that it is ghee with which they are to be anointed (P. M. S. I. 4. 24)

Arthavādas are of three kinds viz. [[gunavāda,|guṇavāda,]] anuvāda and bhūtārthavāda; ‘when an arthavāda text is in conflict with ordinary experience it is metaphorical; when something is definitely ascertained by other means of knowledge and becomes the subject of a text it is called anuvāda and when a text is not opposed to other pramāṇas or is not definitely ascertained otherwise, it is called ‘bhūtārthavāda’ (statement of an accomplished fact, or of a past event); in this way arthavāda is of three kinds’. 65 An example of the first kind is ‘during day-time

[[P1241]]only the smoke of fire is [[geen|seen]] and not its flame’ (this is Tai. Br. II. 1. 2. 10). One sees both smoke and fire during the day and night. What this text desires to emphasize is that by day the light of fire is not so well seen as at night (or not well seen from a distance by day as at night).

‘Fire is the medicine [[(antidote )|(antidote)]] for cold’ ([[Vaj.|Vāj.]] S. 23. 10 and Tai. S. VII. 4. 18. 2) is cited by some as an example of anuvāda. The M. B. P. finds fault with this on the ground that it is well-known as a mantra and does not form part of a vidhi by syntactical relation, and that it is cited by [[Nṛsimhāśrama|Nṛsiṁhāśrama]] as an example owing to neglect of the study of the Veda or absent-mindedness. A proper example would be ‘Vāyu indeed is the swiftest deity’ cited above. ‘Prajāpati cut off his own fat’ is cited by some as an example of bhūtārthavāda, but the M. B. P. does not approve of it and cites the verse ‘yan-na duḥkhena sambhinnam’ (cited above in note 1969) as an example.

The [[Mimāinsāparibhāṣā|Mīmāṁsāparibhāṣā]] of [[Krspayajvan|Kṛṣṇayajvan]] classifies [[Arthavā. das|Arthavādas]] into four kinds, viz. nindā (censure), stuti (praise), parakṛti (performance by another great person) and purākalpa (what

[[P1242]]happened in bygone ages). 66 Devala states that sages have laid down expiation for the first wrong, for committing a second double of that, for committing a third time thrice as much but there is no expiation for committing it a fourth time. The Prāyaścitta-prakaraṇa of Bhavadeva says that this is not to be taken literally, but it is only nindārthavāda. The P. M. S. itself (VI. 7. 26 and 30) states that parakṛti and purākalpa are arthavādas.

The Vyavahāra-mayūkha quotes [[& verge of Devala|a verse of Devala ‘on]] 67 the father’s death the sons should divide the paternal wealth, for they have no ownership as long as the father who suffers from no blemish (that deprive him of ownership) is alive’.
